“Yet in ordinary times, when we are building a democratic government, our admiration for heroes must spread in many directions instead of becoming more intensely concentrated on one person. We must have many heroes until the time comes when all the people of the country become heroes. It is the dignity of the ordinary person that is called democracy. As in America, we must have heroes in fields other than politics—in art, literature, industry, the labour movement and cinema. Here the country’s newspapers can serve by bringing forth new leaders from the new society. At present our newspapers publish the same familiar faces every day. Only the words of the usual few leaders appear.

“Reporters with initiative and enthusiasm will discover that others, too, can feature in the news, and that people will read those reports.

“As democracy takes deeper and deeper root, the disposition to sing the praises of eminent people alone begins to diminish. As the ordinary person’s importance increases, the need to compare that person with ‘big men’ declines. In America, books and periodicals describing how the ordinary person lives are as popular as books describing the deeds of great

### Image 9: 009-5ec155b018.png

men.”

In this article, the scholar has explained that the attitude which insists that only one party should have the opportunity to govern, that even pointing out that party’s mistakes is wrong, however many it makes, and that nothing should be done except to sing its leaders’ praises, leads to dictatorship. He has also explained that, if someone distinguished himself as a great hero in the war of liberation, to insist that he must necessarily remain a great hero in the work of administering the country—and that even if he does not, he must not be censured—will certainly obstruct the country’s progress. Nor was this writer someone who had been running an opposition party against Congress. He said this after studying conditions in the world and methods of governing countries. But Congressmen here have become firmly convinced that the right to rule belongs to them for ever. They are also increasingly determined that, to secure that position, they must use extremely harsh measures now, while they hold power, to make it impossible for any opposition party to Congress to exist. We find their words and actions in keeping with this.

It has now become quite routine for Congress campaigners to say from the platform, “We are going to seize all these people and put them in jail.”

Congressmen now take a new delight in threatening: “There—we have sent a telegram to the minister. Just watch: an arrest warrant will be issued by tomorrow!”

Since they were once subjected to hardship and struck by repression, as soon as they obtain the opportunity to rule they readily develop the desire that others should fear them and that they should subdue and govern others. To ordinary people, this possession of power will seem permanent. Even before a ministry could last out its legally allotted term, a change has already come over the ministry itself, with propaganda fading into darkness and Omandurar


### Image 10: 010-4079e28d08.png

shining forth. If even a ministry is subject to such a change, on what basis can they decide that their position as rulers of the country will last for ever? They do not even consider this.

Repression is a strange power. At first, those who use it experience increasing delight. It is like the pleasure of taking a whip, striking an ox’s back with a sharp crack, and seeing it leap and run faster: when they unleash repression, torment members of the opposition and see them suffer, a kind of delight arises.

Likewise, the first blow brings the person struck great distress, sorrow and bewilderment. The thought arises: “He has power; what can I do against him?”

When the whip has to be used repeatedly, and each time it is used the ox not only leaps but remains quick in its movements, the delight felt at the first stroke withers, and exasperation arises: “What a nuisance this is!”—even though the whip is still in one’s hand. The same is true of those who use repression. The delight they feel when first using it will not recur if they are compelled to use it repeatedly. Weariness will arise, along with the fear: “How long can we sustain our rule through repression alone?”

Likewise, those who were distressed and bewildered when first struck will find that, if they have to endure repeated attacks, their distress and bewilderment burn away; determination and the strength to resist will arise anew.

Repression is a strange kind of power that produces results like these.

“Dying is not so difficult,” a woman said as she died, caught in a tyrant’s clutches. Those words reached her husband’s ears! “Dying is not so difficult! The woman with fish-like eyes said it even as she died! Yet I, afraid of death, even considered surrendering to the

## Part 5

### Image 1: 001-45cc60d8b7.png

tyrant. Shame! I am a coward! This cowardice will not do!” Saying this, he fought furiously and drove the tyrant away, according to a Greek story. In the same way, when the party holding power attacks the opposition through repression, if we withstand that attack, we ourselves shall be amazed at the form of the new strength that subsequently arises.

They have no time to examine this characteristic of repression. That is why they say, “We have set out to govern; henceforth we shall permit no other party to exist in the country.”

Every autocrat in the world has hitherto regarded all who submit to his dominance as good children, and the others as insolent. The Tsar who turned Russia into a battlefield thought in just this way. So do the petty deities who hold trifling powers. They become irritated at the very mention of opposition; arrogance arises, demanding its destruction; and they become ready to employ any cruel, degrading method to destroy it. All these actions, which they regard as brave, are the acts of cowards. In truth, if someone thought that the opposition had no strength and would gain no support in the country, if he believed that his own influence would not diminish however much anyone opposed him, if he had the courage to feel that he could enable the people to understand the truth about him, he would not think of immediately pouncing upon opposition and beating it out of existence as soon as he encountered it.

What befits the brave is to fight without regard for the dangers they face. The cowards’ policy is to tremble at the mere appearance of opposition, fearing, “What will become of us?” and to venture even upon wicked deeds. It is because the former have ultimately won and the latter have finally lost that some measure of justice and integrity, at least, survives in the world today.


### Image 2: 002-85c8f4774c.png

Revolution is a force in motion! No one can burn it out of existence. Revolution arises from the people’s strength to struggle. Revolution is an attribute of youth! The rationalist’s weapon. It is poison to lovers of antiquity, deceivers, autocrats and dictators. That is why such people take fright at the very word “revolution.” When a cyclone comes, even tall trees that have grown to great heights sway, rock, whirl, break at their base, fall and shatter. In the same way, how many autocrats, equipped with all the weapons of armies, treasuries, pride, deceit, arrogance and conceit, and boasting that none was their equal or superior, have stumbled, been battered and rolled to the ground, unable to withstand the force of revolution! That is why all who follow in their footsteps take fright at the mention of revolution. Their fright will not save them. A frightened man will commit many cruel deeds without hesitation. Yet nothing he does will keep him from being caught in the revolutionary storm. This is history.

If autocrats obtain political power, they will use it to try to suppress revolution by law. If they possess military strength, they will shoot revolutionaries dead. If they have neither, and are like the rishis said to have existed in our country in those days, they will pronounce curses. Today Gandhi, regarded as belonging to that “lineage of rishis,” and his priests wield the rod called disciplinary action. Those who have nothing at all try to display their ingenuity in whatever way they can. Did they not think they could suppress opposition to Hindi? Did the priests of the Madurai temple not think that, if they locked the temple doors, hid the keys and went into hiding themselves, temple entry would come to a complete halt? Why are such clownish methods employed? Their mental panic is the reason. Such is the power of revolution.

When the Self-Respect Movement was founded, introduced many new ideas and stirred up revolution,

### Image 3: 003-9f12d76f1c.png

how many people took fright! Were the antics they performed, the uproar they raised and the degrading methods they used because of that fright insignificant?

Likewise, when the revolutionary song “Tamil Nadu for the Tamils” was sung, what people took fright! From the fellow who regarded clowning as a remedy for grievances to the Congress ministers who thought they could burn their opponents with the blaze of their anger, what things they said because of their panic! Revolution frightened their minds in all these ways.

Campaigners and newspapers merely point out the opposition that has arisen against these men in the country; they do not manufacture that opposition themselves. What produces opposition is the consequences of the methods of government, these men’s conduct and speech, new laws and ever-new taxes. These stir up discontent, hatred and anger in the country, gathering and swelling into opposition. Newspapers and campaigns carry the sparks from that “flame” of opposition. Extinguishing the sparks does not amount to extinguishing the flame.

They say that a passage in an Upanishad reads: “Hear the truth, child! Just as sparks fly in all four directions from a great fire blazing with flame…”

There is no use in taking fright at the sparks and destroying them. The “flame” remains, to keep throwing out sparks—countless sparks!

We therefore point out to those who govern that the actions of the panic-stricken will never suppress revolution.


### Image 4: 004-406e80d76d.png

**Here Is the Crux!**

Some friends who are newly hearing about the issues raised by our movement ask whether we criticize Brahmins unnecessarily: they are a very, very small minority in society, so why must we denounce them “at all times, without cease”?

This is an old question for our movement—a very old question. But many who ask it are new “arrivals” to the movement, so they truly ask in order to clear up their doubts. It is our duty to dispel those doubts. First, we tell these friends: please do not imagine that we criticize people called Brahmins merely because of their community, out of malice. What we criticize is a system called Brahminism.

Brahmins are its originators and guardians; many non-Brahmins support it.

Thus our criticism is directed at Brahminism. Now let us examine closely the question: “Brahmins are only a tiny minority! Is it necessary to criticize them and their system?” The problem cannot be understood by considering people’s numbers alone. What chiefly needs consideration is the extent of the influence that the system called Brahminism possesses in the country. Seen in that way, the problem does not stop with Brahmins such as Iyers, Iyengars and Sharmas; we find that it becomes an issue “belonging” to many non-Brahmins too.

When examining a system, it is not enough to consider only the number of people who produced it. What chiefly needs consideration is the extent of the influence that system has acquired in the country. Then the problem’s full dimensions will become apparent.

### Image 5: 005-e9fa7e20ba.png

“Why, sir, fear Brahmins? Why envy them? They are only three out of a hundred! Are you not ninety-seven out of a hundred? Why should the majority community fear a minority community and demand safeguards?” Our nationalist comrades have often asked this. Whenever they do, those comrades congratulate themselves on their own intellectual prowess.

If we consider the community, Brahmins are few in number. The very breath of non-Brahmins would leave Brahmins gasping: that is how numerous non-Brahmins are. But Brahminism does not depend on numbers! It depends on the armed strength with which it has hitherto been furnished in many ways.

A man may be a warrior whom the whole village fears, yet in a moment of delusion he fears the little bunch of neem leaves in a sorcerer’s hand. The greatness imagined to reside in those leaves and in the sorcerer is what creates a situation in which even the warrior is afraid. Likewise, although Brahmins are few in number, countless texts have been written claiming that they possess the keys to salvation and hell, and the measure by which God’s grace is dispensed; that receiving the service of other communities is their birthright; and that their satisfaction gives satisfaction to God himself. Because this notion has been mixed into the people’s blood over a very long time, a way has been opened for that community to acquire immense influence, wholly out of proportion to its numbers.

The bridle is small, but everyone knows what the horse suffers under it! The rope through a bull’s nose is not even as thick as its tail. Yet once caught by it, the animal submits! In the same way, however small a community may be, if various kinds of greatness are attributed to it, its members’ influence will certainly grow. If an aeroplane circles over a village, its people are frightened even if they number 9,000. Why? Because they know that bombs will be dropped from the aeroplane. This is why the problem called Brahminism must be criticized.


### Image 6: 006-fb11851fab.png

Has not Brahminism acquired enough “power” to make so many non-Brahmins ask, “Why, sir, should Brahmins be criticized?” When Brahminism is criticized, even the Anandacharis may remain quiet, while it is the Avinashis who become angry. Why? That is the crux. Although they are a very, very small minority, their system has spread so widely and acquired such influence. That is why our movement regards this issue as important. Thus far, the objections and resistance to the opposition we have created against Brahminism have come more from our own people than from Brahmins. We ask the friends who raise these questions to consider: is criticizing so powerful a system useless work? Is it unnecessary for us to work courageously against a system possessing such influence?

**Thoothukudi**

Iron chains on his arms!

His legs, too, have been chained with iron to an immovable rock.

A heavy cloth is bound over his eyes.

On seeing him, one understands: “He is an unfortunate man, bound fast to suffering—the chains of slavery subject him to cruelty.”

Few people pass through the place around him. In the distance, the sound of waves is heard.

A fierce wind blows.

Rain pours down too.

He writhes—

### Image 7: 007-b34a652c44.png

There is no one to speak on his behalf!

There is no one with the decency to undo his bonds.

There is no brave man to ask why he is in this condition. He writhes—held down by a strength greater than his own, he can only writhe.

Will help come? Will he hear a reassuring voice? He listens intently, eagerly. He lies waiting to hear a footstep in the distance.

He waits eagerly for a loving word, an expression of compassion, a word to give him hope.

He wonders whether someone might at least remove the piece of cloth covering his eyes. He writhes—writhes—and, as he writhes, thinks: will someone not say, “A little more force—with a little more determination—without growing weary—without losing hope, try to free yourself from these bonds! The chains may be iron, but you have a heart of steel. Do not fear! Look, the moment when the fetters will break has arrived. Brave man, do not despair!” He waits longingly for someone to say this and kindle his spirits. Only the fierce wind blows! Only the sound of the waves is heard! The voice of help he awaits does not arise. Far away, some people travel in pretty little boats; the sindhu song they sing reaches his ears.

From somewhere else comes a rumbling sound. But no one comes near him! We see no one who dares to change his condition. He is a slave! He is a sufferer! There is nothing to support him!!

Thirty years ago, the Dravidian was in this condition. Bound by the fetters called caste and religion, chained to the rock called Aryanism, blindfolded with the piece of cloth called ignorance, in the days when the world was changing,


### Image 8: 008-115bf6aae7.png

when the cries of struggles for rights were being heard in many places, he floundered, not knowing how to free himself, finding no companion to help him, battered by a strength greater than his own. A fetter more terrible than those on his arms and legs bound his mind! Nor was it a fetter suddenly fastened upon him. Taking advantage of his innocent heart, people with deceitful intentions had slowly fastened these fetters upon him many centuries before. Like a little bird wandering bewildered in a forest where it cannot find its bearings, like a boatman distressed because no harbour can be found whichever way he goes, he suffered in his bonds.

Because this condition had continued for so long, he had lost his self-confidence too.

He even writhed less. He began trying to stop thinking of what had been fastened upon him as fetters. Having tried and failed to see through the piece of cloth covering his eyes, he had come to doubt the very idea that eyes could see. He had even concluded: “There is no freedom for us, no deliverance. These are unbreakable fetters. This is a condition from which there is no escape.”

In this Tamil land, one man alone found the courage to say, “This condition can be changed—it is possible to change it—I shall show you by changing it!” He began the work, assembled an army, tore away the piece of cloth over the eyes and began smashing the fetters too.

At first, the man who had lost hope was simply alarmed! “You have come forward to do something impossible! Previously my eyes were covered. I merely knew there were fetters; I did not know what they were like. My eyes have been opened. Now I can see that the fetters are very, very strong. How can they be smashed? You have no sledgehammer! You are smashing them with your bare hands,

### Image 9: 009-bdee30b56d.png

and suffering pain as a result. Poor man! This is beyond your power. Give up the attempt! It is enough that my eyes have somehow been opened. I see the sky, I see the birds flying through it, I see the waves, and I rejoice. This is enough. These fetters will not break under your blows,” he said.

“Yes—yes—strong fetters,” the other man replied.

“Not merely strong fetters. They were not fastened today or yesterday, two years or ten years ago. These fetters have been here for ages,” said the man in bonds.

With a smile, the other man said: “Only now does my confidence grow. These fetters were fastened a very long time ago! You say this is their greatness. Fool! Think properly. This is nothing to boast of! The fetters are very, very old, and therefore weak! They have been here for a very long time, and therefore, under the assault of time, have gradually lost their strength. They are not unbreakable. If we strive with vigour and confidence, we can shatter them to dust,” he declared firmly. From somewhere came the sound of someone laughing mockingly. Disregarding it, he continued the work of smashing the fetters—tirelessly, successfully, for thirty years. The sound of the fetters breaking grows strong enough to drown out the mocking laughter!! Confidence grows! A new light shines from the eyes that had grown dim.

The Dravidar Kazhagam provincial conference held at Thoothukudi last year was the loving tribute of an awakened Dravidam, now filled with confidence, to Periyar, who has been carrying out this great work with vigour!

Men and women, old people and children—more than a hundred thousand—gathered at the southern tip of Dravidam and celebrated.

At a time when the members of parties that include ministers and statesmen have reached the stage of searching for recruits, here,


### Image 10: 010-656546af35.png

a hundred thousand people gather for the Dravidar Kazhagam, which states with complete clarity that it has no intention even of looking toward the seat of power! They experience a stirring of the heart like that of a son entering his home after returning from a long stay abroad. They rejoice as though seeing a treasury discovered after a long search. A kind of affection, an attachment, binds them together and sets them in motion.

> In Tamil Nadu fallen low, there must be an awakening;
> In the body whose vigour has failed, there must be strength.

The poem becomes a visible scene, and awakening walks abroad. A new vigour enters their thinking! The conviction that their ideal will somehow be fulfilled mingles with their flesh and life, making them new people. Even if they expect this or that to happen and it does not, there is no room for anxiety; happiness arises.

The fools who babble, “With the hand that holds grass, we shall wield a sword,” should consider what this “situation” signifies, what it symbolizes and what lesson it teaches. Their minds narrowed by arrogance, these grass-bearers fail to understand it. We urge them to think: what does this enthusiasm mean? It means that a people has awakened! It means that the tiger has awakened! In this situation, what do they expect to accomplish by wielding a sword in the hand that holds grass?

If they learn nothing even from this great conference, which made plain the course of the times and the new thinking that has arisen among a people, we shall say that the claim that they are exceptionally intelligent is also absurd. Only an exceedingly low mentality and an exceedingly poor intellect could produce the audacity to say, “With the hand that holds grass, we shall wield a sword.” The Thoothukudi conference is a beautiful picture illustrating how ridiculous this “audacity”—this outlook combining ignorance and arrogance—is!

## Part 6

### Image 1: 001-2fe6671b39.png

Thus the conference’s distinction does more than bring joy to Kazhagam members: it is a warning of danger to the dull-witted who imagine that the country will continue to accommodate their conceit. Only when one understands that the great gathering assembled there that day had renounced the kinds of “desires and attachments” in which parties usually become entangled—that is, political gambling and the hunt for office—can one understand the nature of the strength that has grown and what its consequences will be. The wearers of the three-stranded thread boast that they understand all the inner meaning of the Vedas; yet they still do not seem to have understood this truth alone. Had they understood it, *Bharata Devi* would not have spewed its old madness in its issue of the 11th, nor would another wearer of the three-stranded thread have babbled, “With the hand that holds grass, we shall wield a sword.” Their madness has not diminished because they still fail to understand the country’s present condition. Let the Thoothukudi conference, at least, bring them clarity.

The great lesson we learned at the Salem conference was the principle that, if we become bound to vulgarities and engage in political gambling, our growing strength will scatter and disintegrate. The leader has made clear that the Kazhagam will continue on the same course. It is therefore clear that the Kazhagam will not be sacrificed to the political silkworms and grasshoppers that fly in to settle upon it when they see it flourishing green.

Having cut away vulgarity and increased its enthusiasm, it has advanced from the condition of wondering whether anyone would attend a gathering to one in which people joyfully estimate, “The crowd will be a hundred thousand; it will be two hundred thousand.” It has escaped the accusation, “All this is for elections, office, titles and contracts,” and advanced to a position where it can be said, “This is to reshape society and bring about a revolution in the realm of knowledge.” Anyone who disregards this great strength can only be a mean-spirited person, incapable of discerning and explaining the direction of people’s minds. “All right;


### Image 2: 002-278c27cd33.png

at the next election, I shall get rid of these creatures, even if I have to spend half a crore or a crore,” is not something they can say. Nor is there room to expose them by declaring, “These are selfish office-seekers! Look, this person and that person are grinning eagerly for such-and-such posts,” and then boast, “We shall make it impossible for these creatures to show their faces outside.”

Nor is there room for the manoeuvre, “We shall give high office to those who shout loudly and turn them into our favourites.” For the Dravidar Kazhagam stands beyond such desires and attachments. The gold refined at Salem has become gold of undiminished purity. The Muthanna crowd, “born from the face,” should give some thought to this great truth.

The Kazhagam has left no room, either, for the treacherous thought: “Wait, wait! We shall set Congress against these creatures and rejoice to see blood pouring from both their heads.” For it has reached a position in which it has entirely renounced the idea of competing with the Congress party in elections or for posts.

That is why we say that the condition of having severed desires and attachments gives such great vitality.

Those who formerly held grass and now imagine they might wield a bow must also abandon the thought that they can derive satisfaction by saying, “Leave aside the Kazhagam’s aims and its position; are not division and dissension increasing within it?” Differences exist. But those differences merely give both the differing sections an opportunity to count the people between them and understand their character; they will not serve in the slightest to give the enemy an opening. The differences are like a competitive contest through which we discover how particular people will use particular opportunities. They will in no way produce a disposition to make room for opponents.

Therefore, both those who divided the country into four castes and those who govern

### Image 3: 003-cc6596e983.png

must understand the lesson of the Thoothukudi conference. All that was in the hearts of the people gathered there was this:

> A people free from caste distinctions and economic inequality, clear in understanding and not subjected to exploitation by an outsider, must establish a government exercising its rightful authority and take its seat in the assembly of the world.

This fundamental idea can no longer be defeated, nor can its growth be stopped. It has not remained confined to the Kazhagam’s precincts. It is penetrating khadi shirts and finding a place in the hearts of our Congress friends. It emerges in the speeches of thoughtful people in the country. In new poems, new plays and new films alike, this fundamental idea has begun to appear clearly. We therefore ask them to abandon the dream that they can destroy it and, before the blows of time fall heavily, to clarify their thinking and set about establishing justice. Before the very eyes of those who thought, “We have fastened iron chains upon his hands and his mind; how can that slave free himself?” the Dravidian now strides proudly forward, without even having cast off the broken fetters, stands before them and declares:

> This is my country! Nature will make it a golden land! There must be no caste here! No cruelty of religion, no economic inequality! No exploitation by the North! We want rights! We want a new life!

Into the ears of the Dravidian, who had listened in distress to the wind and the sea, came the fervour and solemn vow of a hundred thousand people! He walks the path of victory. That journey cannot be stopped; that victory cannot be spoiled. He has awakened and risen; he appears like a lion that has emerged from its cage. Those who boast that the wearers of the three-stranded thread possess an “eye of wisdom” need not even use that eye of wisdom,


### Image 4: 004-79c4fc2b98.png

for ordinary physical sight and average intelligence are enough to see the truth that cannot be concealed!

**Last Will and Testament**

This is the last will and testament that every comrade in the Dravidar Kazhagam must prepare. We have brought about the awakening of a fallen people; we have reached the stage at which we must secure liberation, whatever the price.

Slashing and stabbing will now come swiftly in search of us; we shall have to move upon the swing suspended between life and death. That is the lesson taught us by the disturbance on the banks of the Vaigai last week. Varnashrama, knowing beyond doubt that our continued existence threatens its dominance, and knowing well that only if we die can it survive even a little longer, performed a horrific dance to kill us. It drank a little of our blood and tasted it. The signs of our growth are known not only to us but also to the Brahmana Seva Sangam. Their petition is the testimonial that Aryanism gives to our growth.

“All that is well, but harm occurs in such a righteous struggle. Blood is being wasted,” some think. A word to them! They should reflect for a moment on the nature of the great undertaking we have assumed.

We wish to reform arrangements said to have existed for age upon age. We are blasting the mountain of conceit. If small fragments fly down upon us and split our skulls, it means that the blast we set is splitting the mountain. Having undertaken to split the mountain,

### Image 5: 005-9b05d38fd0.png

we cannot expect flowers to fall upon our heads, can we? We have voluntarily committed ourselves to this work. The sufferings endured by those who set about comparable work in many parts of the world have now become university textbooks. The poison Socrates drank then has given him immortality to this day. We have joined the lineage of those who take accusation, humiliation, opposition and danger upon their heads, carry out their work and pass on, leaving later generations to enjoy its fruits. We shall certainly face hardship and obstruction, but our labour will never be in vain!

Killed with deadly poison; consigned to dark prisons for their entire lives; stoned and driven away; crucified; made prey for leopards; strangled; thrown into fire; hacked to pieces; hounded out by their own countrymen; left without anyone to turn to—such will be the life histories of those who engaged in the work of social revolution. We belong to that kind. Today they have all become jewels of the intellectual world. Future generations will not forget us either.

There were times when molten lead was poured into ears; when people were tied up and their houses set on fire; when they were thrown into wells and stones cast upon them; when eyes were gouged out; tongues severed; bodies impaled; heads cut off; and people thrown into burning coals. Those who spoke of reform suffered amid these things. Most did not simply die; they were killed. It is because they were killed that they live undying today. That is why we say that we must prepare our last wills and testaments and enter this great struggle.

We who desire fundamental change in society may hold back, thinking, “Why should we undertake this work?” because we might be killed; even then, “death” will not take


### Image 6: 006-33c8ef88ab.png

a rest. Even if we speak of peace, the hymn will still sound in our ears: “Alas, there is no certainty even to think that those here today will be here tomorrow!” If we die, we are dead! But if we are killed while doing our duty, that is to receive the boon of immortality! We shall live. In the speech and songs of the generations after us, who gain a new life through the fruits of our labour, we shall be spoken of—beside the cradle, beside the flower garden, beside the bed and in the debating hall. When they say, “They worked for us and were killed,” it will mean that we are still alive. At the approach of death, will we be troubled by thoughts of what will happen to the mansion, what will become of the beautiful women, what fate awaits the gardens and wells, who will get the earrings and pendants, whether trade will decline or interest income be lost? No! We shall die saying, “Aryanism! You have killed me! You did this knowing that my being alive was a danger to you. Do not be satisfied! Turn and look! Many others remain who have not become corpses.” Therefore, prepare your last wills and testaments!

The week after Madurai, our comrades gathered in Kudanthai in their many thousands. There were far more women here than at Madurai. Conferences on two days, plays on both nights: the extent and character of the awakening were such as to make one wonder whether the Madurai incident had been a scene seen in a dream. What does this mean? It means that our comrades have shown that our great movement will not fade because of the Madurai incident. Thousands of young people full of enthusiasm and fervour gathered there, some with scars from Madurai. They proclaimed that they were ready to work. Madurai has fallen quiet. The hands of those who threw stones at our comrades may not yet have stopped aching. Yet our comrades, who received blows from stones and wounds from knives, gathered at Kudanthai to work as usual even before their wounds had healed. Yes! They have undertaken this great work after preparing their last wills and testaments. We commend their courage, their spirit of sacrifice and their determination. We bow our heads before their courage. Comrades who shed blood upon the sand! Your blood was not shed in vain.

### Image 7: 007-73675630df.png

That news has set the blood of countless Tamils boiling. It has given them a state of mind that sees no distinction between life and death. It has inspired the thought: “We may be killed; therefore, while we are here today, let us do a little more work for our people’s awakening.” The righteous struggle to protect the country’s language and culture has swept away even the little family affection and attachment that remained among our people. All that remains now is ourselves, our service, Aryanism writhing at the sight of it, the weapon it launches, and the urgency of doing our work before that weapon strikes—nothing more! We have prepared our last wills and testaments and entered this great struggle! Therefore it is certain that our ideal—our objective—must ultimately triumph!

## Translator's Notes

All six parts and 58 scans are translated. Printed dates, historical claims, reported speeches and polemical comparisons are retained as the source presents them. Scan boundaries remain visible; English sentences may continue across them. The notes below distinguish interpretation from changes to the Tamil, which is retained verbatim.

- Part 1 Images 2–4: the opening statement is attributed at its end to Leader Periyar. Its claims about self-rule and the departure of British domination are translated as the statement's claims; they are not editorial corrections to the printed 29-6-1947 date. The apparent chronological tension remains in the source.
- `பிரச்சார இயக்கம்` is rendered “movement for spreading/propagating its ideas,” with ordinary English variation. The contrast is between an ideological/social movement and a party seeking electoral office, not between honest and deceptive communication.
- `தெளிவுபடுத்தி ஆய்விட்டது` is the unusual printed form already preserved in OCR recovery. “Already made this clear” conveys the contextual function without silently repairing the Tamil.
- `தாழ்த்தப்பட்டவர்கள், ஷெடியூல் லீக்` is retained as “the Depressed Classes, the Scheduled League,” following the printed list. No different official organizational name is substituted.
- The opening quotation on Image 2 and renewed quotation on Image 4 have no corresponding closing quotation marks before the attribution in the corrected source. The English retains this typographical irregularity; it does not imply an omitted passage.
- `ஒரு மூச்சுப் பார்த்தாக` is rendered “put that strength to the test,” preserving the call for a concerted effort. `சூத்திரர்` is “Shudras,” the caste designation repudiated by the speaker.
- `அதல சுதல பாதாளம்` names mythological lower worlds. “Cosmic falsehoods” preserves the polemical force of `அண்டம் புளுகு`; the translation does not present the author's judgment as editorial narration outside the work.

- Part 1 Image 5: `மனத்தை` is printed, rather than the expected `மானத்தை`; “their spirit” follows the retained word without silently changing it to “honour.” The claims about ancient Dravidam are translated as the author's rhetorical contrasts, not independently established historical findings.
- Part 1 Images 5–6: “Dravidam” is retained as the geographical/cultural name. “Seth” retains the merchant honorific; “Bania,” “Marwari,” “Gujarati” and “Multani” retain the source's community/regional labels and polemical generalizations. “Asher” transliterates the printed `ஆஷர்`; no unverified corporate identification is substituted.
- Part 1 Image 6: “The bell that rings every day” preserves the wording and possible newspaper-name wordplay in `தினம் ஒலிக்கும் மணி`; Goenka's name is retained without adding a publication name absent from this sentence. `கட்டியம் கூற` is rendered “singing the praises,” conveying the heraldic/deferential image.
- Image 6 ends with `நஞ்சை`, joined to `புஞ்சையையும்` on Image 7. English continues “irrigated fields here and” / “dry fields...” at the scan boundary.

- Part 1 Images 7–8: the chronology follows the corrected source's separated column blocks and retains their nonchronological order. All dates, including 20-8-1944 and 1-7-1947, are translated as printed; they are not silently harmonized with other historical chronologies or the opening date.
- `பரணி பாடினோம்` is “sang our battle song,” retaining the martial literary image rather than merely saying “announced.” “Dravida Nadu” is used for the named separation programme in the chronology; “a Dravidian country” renders the accompanying prose demand.
- Image 8 prints `சொன்னோம்` above both opposed statements. Both are retained as “We Said”. The attribution `ஜவஹர்` is retained as “Jawahar.”
- The Pakistan/graveyard phrase preserves the force of `பாகிஸ்தான் ... கபர்ஸ்தான்`. The connecting printed `இன்` is irregular; “or” renders the clear choice in context and is an interpretive connection, not a Tamil correction. The malformed `ஆபசமானது` and `பார்க்கு மனவு` in the opposing quotation are also left unchanged in Tamil; the English conveys the contextual sense “dangerous” and “worthy of consideration.”
- “Pakistan has been obtained” is the source's own retrospective claim; it is not rewritten to resolve the chronology's relation to the opening 29 June 1947 date.

- Part 1 Image 9: `தெ. இ. ந. உ. சங்கம்` is expanded by its Tamil components as “South Indian Welfare Rights Association”; the source uses initials, not an English institutional name. The expansion is descriptive, not a quoted official English name. The Salem date is “the 27th” as printed; it differs from the chronology's 20-8-1944.
- `விவேக சிந்தாமணி` remains the work title *Viveka Chintamani*. The contrast is between scholarly exposition or political manoeuvring and the liberation struggle; no added description of that book's contents is needed.
- Part 1 Image 10: the solitary asterisk following the first paragraph is retained; no corresponding footnote is supplied on this scan. `சாவு ஓலை` becomes “summons of death,” preserving the written-message image. `மனப்பால் குடித்து` becomes “indulge the fancy,” not a literal claim about milk.
- The Tamil's historical achievements and generalizations about communities remain the author's rhetoric. `பொன்னாடை` is “golden cloth”; `கடாரம்` remains “Kadaram” without silently identifying a modern territory. The title question continues from Image 10's soap into Image 11's combs and mirrors.

- Part 1 Image 11: the Shelley quotation is translated from the Tamil wording, not silently replaced with an English edition's wording. The comparison “Britain's Bharathidasan” belongs to the author. `தளபதி பாண்டியன்` is retained as “Commander Pandian,” without expanding the personal name beyond the source.
- Part 2 Image 1: a fresh visual check confirms the anomalous printed `வரு நிரம்பிய உடலும்`. “A body full of vigour” is a contextual interpretation, possibly pointing to intended `வலு`; it is uncertain and is not a correction to the Tamil. The interpretation remains uncertain. `வைரம் பாய்ந்த உள்ளம்` becomes “a heart of adamant,” preserving hardness/resolve rather than inventing literal diamonds.
- `தாலமுத்து நடராஜன்கள்` is rendered as the pluralized names “Thalamuthus and Natarajans,” retaining the invocation of exemplary people without adding biographical claims. The source's military language—commanders, camp, fighters and battle—is retained as movement rhetoric.
- The Part 1/Part 2 boundary falls inside a sentence: “It is to” / “accomplish this...” Image 1 of Part 2 itself ends “not to us,” to be completed from the next scan.

- Part 2 Images 2–3: the numbered reasons are translated as the author's political argument. Figures (32 European countries, 56 pre-British countries and 574 princely states), the designation “Dutch India,” and the threefold classification of peoples are retained as printed rather than corrected by reference to a modern historical account.
- `இனம்` in this argument is rendered “people/peoples,” and `இன இயல்புகள்` as “collective characteristics.” The author defines these through religion, language, art, outlook, shared belonging and history; the translation does not add a biological definition. “According to its peoples” renders `இனவாரியாக` consistently in these reasons.
- `சுதேச இந்தியா` is rendered “princely India” in the list of jurisdictions, following the immediately subsequent discussion of princely states. `இரவல் பொருள்` is “borrowed resources”; no specific debt arrangement is inferred.
- Reason 2 crosses Images 2–3. Reason 7 ends on Image 3 and reason 8 begins on Image 4.

- Part 2 Images 4–5: reasons 8–15 complete the numbered argument. Statements about Turkey, the ancient/medieval extent of India, British coercion and Pakistan are retained as the author's claims, without silently updating or harmonizing them.
- `பூதேவர்கள்` is “gods on earth,” retaining the polemical description of Brahmin social status. `வர்க்கத்துக்கொரு` in reason 13 is “for each group,” following the surrounding argument about peoples rather than imposing a separate modern class-theory meaning.
- `வலுத்தவனுக்கு வாழ்க்கைப்படும் வனிதை` is translated with its gendered marital metaphor intact. It is the author's simile for subjection to the strongest, not an editorial description added by the translator.
- Reason 15's throat-crushing sentence crosses Images 4–5. The corrected Tamil's unusual `அழித்து நெரித்துக்` is rendered “crushed ... and strangled”; the Tamil remains untouched. The final `இன அரசு` is “a state for their people,” consistent with this section's rendering of `இனம்`.

- Part 2 Image 6: `போர்வாள்` is treated as the section heading “The Battle Sword.” “The following week” retains the relative timing without supplying an event date absent from the passage. The bare `2000` remains “two thousand,” without adding an unprinted currency.
- `தங்கக்கட்டிகள்` affectionately describes the mothers' babies as treasures; `ஏணைகள்` are suspended cloth cradles. `மிட்டா மிராசுகள்` is rendered collectively as “great landed proprietors”; `ஜெமீன்` retains the zamindar context. The anomalous printed `திருஇடம்` is rendered “land” in context, without rewriting the Tamil to திராவிடம்.
- Part 2 Image 7: `தீண்டினால் திருநீலகண்டம்` invokes a religious oath/taboo against touching; “not to be touched—by Tiruneelakantam!” retains the allusion with this explanation. `காவடி தூக்குவோர்` is rendered “bearing the burdens,” retaining its service/burden image in the sarcastic political context.
- Quoted songs are translated from the supplied Tamil; no remembered or externally sourced continuation is added. The violent sword imagery remains a quotation within this historical text. `பெரியோரே` is “the great elder,” following the printed form. `கடும் பத்தியம்` conveys severe restrictions or discipline; it is not limited here to dietary rules.
- The grove/prison contrast is retained as presented. The final sentence crosses Images 7–8: “Inadequate railway facilities,” followed by the weather difficulties and the assembled crowd.

- Part 2 Image 8: the long sequence of “after” clauses is retained as a rhetorical accumulation. `ஞாயிறு` is “the sun,” preserving the metaphor rather than replacing it with a person's name. The unusual `சுனை விழுங்கிகள்` in the burrow-creature insult is not securely interpretable; “sunai-vizhungis, wriggling in their holes” retains the epithet in transliteration and translates the clear surrounding image. Its precise idiomatic meaning remains unresolved; Tamil is unchanged.
- `ஜல்லடம்` is rendered “fighting trunks” in the mock-heroic context. `சர். சண்முகனார்` remains “Sir Shanmuganar,” and the description as a Silappathikaram commentator is the source's own. No biographical expansion is added.
- Part 2 Image 9: `கோடி உள்ளங்கள்` becomes “ten million hearts,” preserving the rhetorical quantity. Atana is retained as the musical name. The yellow-to-black lyric is translated literally without supplying an unprinted explanation of party colours. `கனங்கள்` is rendered “‘Honourables’” with the ironic quotation marks intact.
- `தாட்சணியம்` is “consideration for others,” in the adhesive metaphor continuing onto Image 10. The inability to chew or swallow is retained as an idiom for Periyar's predicament. The text's quoted ‘Salem’ denotes the earlier-described party transformation; no extra event date is inserted.

- Part 2 Image 10: `தேர்தல் காளான்` is kept as “electoral mushroom,” the author's dismissive image of a rapidly assembled electoral formation. `பில்லைச் சேவகர்கள்` is rendered “liveried servants,” preserving the badge/retainer implication rather than an unmarked modern “assistants.”
- The later reference to people watching and enjoying `போர்வாள்` treats it as a performance title, *The Battle Sword*. The earlier section heading uses the same English title. No playwright, production date or additional stage detail is inferred.
- The Panagal quotation crosses the Part 2/Part 3 boundary and is kept continuous. The quotations attributed to Achariar are translated as reported in the source, not presented as independently authenticated transcripts. `வேதியர்` is “Brahmin” here, following its polemical personal reference.
- Part 3 Image 1: the printed name `நடராசன்` is transliterated “Natarasan,” while the preceding part's `நடராஜன்` was “Natarajan”; the source variation is not silently normalized. `புரட்சிக் கவிஞர்` is “the revolutionary poet,” with no personal name added. The final accumulating conditional sentence continues onto Image 2.

- Part 3 Image 2: `திருநாமம்` is the sacred forehead mark; “‘Brahmin pollution’” preserves the deliberate inversion in `பார்ப்பனத் தீட்டு`. “Gandhi's relative by marriage” follows `சம்பந்தியார்` without adding an unprinted personal name.
- The military comparisons, including the attribution to Wavell and the claim about seizing Pearl Harbor, are translated as the author's rhetoric, not silently corrected against military history. `மேற்கு அரசாங்கத்திலும் ... கிழக்கு அரசாங்கத்திலும்` is rendered “in the Western governments ... Eastern governments,” retaining the unusual printed governmental wording rather than substituting “fronts.”
- Part 3 Image 3: `ஏகபோக மிராசு பாத்யதை` becomes “exclusive proprietary right,” preserving the landed-property metaphor. `சமதர்மம்` is “socialism” in the jab at paper-only adherents; this does not add a party affiliation.
- The source explicitly explains the black shirt as mourning for social degradation. Its reference to fascism is an anticipated accusation by opponents, and is translated in that attributed form. The final sentence continues “social” / “degradation” across Images 3–4.

- Part 3 Images 4–5: the temple-entry debate distinguishes equal access as a right from endorsement of temple religion. The translation preserves both the radical objections voiced by delegates and the explanation on which the resolution was ultimately adopted.
- `ஆதித்திராவிடர்` remains “Adi Dravidians”; `பழங்குடி மக்கள்` is “original inhabitants” in this passage, without substituting a modern administrative category. `சனாதனம்` and `வைதீகம்` are rendered contextually as religious orthodoxy and ritualism; the hostile fortress, thieves' den and trap images remain attributed to the quoted speakers.
- T. Shanmugam, Neelamegam and Arjunan retain their printed names and specified conference roles. No external biographical identification is added. The signboard passage crosses Images 4–5; the sentence about Vedachalam's efforts crosses Images 5–6.

- Part 3 Image 6: Periyar becoming forty-five after arriving at sixty-seven is an explicit image of rejuvenation through the conference, not an age correction. The sugarcane-joint comparison is preserved as a metaphor for difficulties encountered within an otherwise rewarding endeavour.
- The final battle-sword paragraph targets figurative cactus, poisonous vines, thorny caste divisions and antiquated custom. These metaphors are retained; the translation does not turn them into added instructions about physical attacks.
- Part 3 Image 7: musical `சுருதி` is “underlying pitch,” conveying the organizing reference to which the other programmes are aligned. `சில்லரைகள்` is “small change,” preserving the dismissive image of concessions short of the ideal.
- The heading about Natesa Mudaliar is translated as printed; the prose says he proclaimed the cause until his last breath and the next scan says he died broken-hearted. The heading alone does not establish a violent death. His name is retained as Dr C. Natesa Mudaliar.
- Image 7 ends inside the sentence about the palace politicians' intended use of their abilities; the following scan completes that intention before assessing its consequences.

- Part 3 Images 8–9: `மாயமான்` remains the “illusory deer,” preserving the repeated pursuit metaphor rather than reducing it to “ambition.” `ஆள் தூக்கிகள்` becomes “promoters of personalities” and `சூத்திரதாரிகள்` “puppet-masters,” retaining the political manipulation described.
- S. I. L. F. remains the printed English initials; *Dravidan* is the Tamil newspaper title. Personal names retain the source's initials and forms. “Iyers” retains the narrower label used in the government-employment sentence, while “Brahmins” translates the broader label elsewhere.
- `தனித்தமிழ்`, `வேளாள நாகரிகம்` and `உண்மைச் சைவம்` are “Pure Tamil,” “Vellala civilization” and “true Saivism.” These are the author's labels for movements, not independent judgments of cultural authenticity. `அரசியல் வெட்டுக்கிளிகள்` remains “political grasshoppers.”
- The people’s reaction crosses Images 8–9. The final sentence about hostility between movements continues from Image 9 onto Image 10.

- Part 3 Image 10: the irrigation-channel and withering-crop heading is retained as a metaphor for reviving Tamil consciousness. “Five Great Epics” translates the named literary grouping without importing a list absent from the passage. The printed irregular `மாற்றால்` in the heading is understood contextually as opponents, matching `மாற்றார்கள்` in the following paragraph; Tamil remains unchanged.
- Part 4 Image 1: the crowd is said to approach a lakh, rendered “a hundred thousand.” The speech and crowd response are translated as the author's account. `கரைகள்` in the cloud comparison is rendered “blemishes” contextually; the anomalous printed form is not silently changed to கறைகள். “It reached them” makes explicit the inferred peopleward movement in the elliptical `போய்ச் சேர்ந்தது`; this connection is interpretive.
- `பள்ளு` remains “pallu song,” preserving the Tamil song-form reference. The quoted couplet is translated solely from the retained Tamil, without substituting another lyric. The sentence listing elections, legislatures and ministries continues onto Part 4 Image 2.

- Part 4 Image 2: the historical, geographical and cultural claims remain the author's argument. `மக்கள் உளப்பண்பு நூல்` is “the study of people's mental characteristics,” without assigning it an unprinted modern disciplinary name.
- Part 4 Image 3: `திராவிடஸ்தான்` is retained as “Dravidastan,” distinct from the usual Dravida Nadu. The embedded English INDEPENDENT UNITS is retained and followed by a translation of the Tamil gloss. `சமஷ்டி` is “federation.”
- The heading about self-interest is rendered as printed, although its opening quotation mark is unpaired. The final question is completed syntactically in English at the end of Image 3; Image 4 begins with the source's final question words and then “He did not explain this.” The question is translated once.

- Part 4 Image 4: `அ. நி. சபை` is expanded contextually as the Constituent Assembly. The wordplay between `அங்கங்கள்` (units) and `பங்கப்படுத்தப்படுகின்றன` (being impaired) is conveyed in meaning; the English does not reproduce its sound. `சல்லடம்` in the heading evokes clothing worn for a physical contest and is rendered “gird ourselves for the contest,” retaining the figurative challenge.
- Images 4–5: the list of commercial goods and places is the author's economic argument. Printed `விஷாவார்` is retained as “Vishawar,” without silently substituting a modern place-name. `வங்காள ரசாயண சாலை` is translated descriptively as “Bengal chemical works,” without asserting a specific corporate identity. The Sholapur–Bombay sentence crosses the scan boundary.
- Image 5: “attitude of worship” retains `பூஜா மனோபாவம்` and its criticism of political reverence. Quotation marks delimit the preceding Congress position introduced by the author's “had been saying”; the English punctuation makes that attribution explicit. The final conditional sentence continues onto Image 6.

- Part 4 Images 6–7: the quoted commentary is attributed in the source to Dr `கிருஷ்ணலால் சீதரணி`; “Krishnalal Seetharani” follows that printed name rather than silently substituting a different spelling. English quotation marks repeat at paragraph openings to show that the lengthy quotation remains in progress.
- Image 6: `குடி அரசுக் கோட்பாடு` is “principle of republican government”; the following `ஜனநாயகம்` is “democracy.” The split into right and left wings crosses Images 6–7 and is syntactically completed in Image 6; Image 7 begins with the ensuing qualification. `இடைக் கட்சி` is interpreted as “party of the centre” in this right–left context.
- Image 7: `தன் குறும்புத்தனத்தை அதாவது எதேச்சதிகாரத்தை` has an unclear reflexive referent. The English retains “mischief—that is, autocracy” without supplying a named actor absent from the sentence. The neem-fruit comparison preserves the bitterness metaphor. The claim that press independence is harder under native rule belongs to the quoted commentator.

- Part 4 Images 8–9: `வீரர் வணக்கம்` is “hero-worship”; where the passage advocates dispersed admiration, the English also uses “admiration for heroes.” The quotation closes in Image 9 after the comparison of American publications. The subsequent critique and reported threats return to the author's voice.
- Image 8: `படே மனிதர்கள்` is rendered “big men,” retaining the colloquial emphasis. Claims about American readership are retained as the quoted writer's claims.
- Image 9: `பிரசாரம் இருண்டு` is rendered literally as “propaganda fading into darkness.” The transition to Omandurar may suggest an intended personal-name contrast, but the retained source reads `பிரசாரம்`; the English does not silently replace it with a different Tamil reading or an inferred person's name. Direct visual reinspection of the scan confirms the printed form `பிரசாரம்`; this remains a textual concern rather than an OCR correction. The sentence continues onto Image 10.

- Part 4 Image 10: `துரிதமாக இருந்தால்` is rendered “remains quick in its movements.” Its place in the ox-and-whip comparison is awkward; the English retains the printed sense of speed rather than silently substituting defiance or another conjectural reading. Repression, the whip and the ox remain the author's extended political comparison.
- The Greek story crosses the Part 4–5 boundary. No names or external identification are supplied for its unnamed woman, husband or tyrant. `சேல்விழியாள்` becomes “the woman with fish-like eyes,” retaining the conventional poetic description within the husband's speech.
- Part 5 Image 1: `சிறு தேவதைகள்` remains “petty deities,” the author's metaphor for minor holders of power. The account of the Tsar and the claims about bravery, cowardice and justice remain the author's argument, without added historical assertions.

- Part 5 Images 2–3: the repeated sound contrast `புரட்சி` / `மருட்சி` is conveyed as revolution versus fright or panic. The storm, burning, rod and sparks are retained as the author's political imagery. `புரட்டர்கள்` is “deceivers”; `ரிஷிகள்` remains “rishis.” The description of Gandhi's followers as priests belongs to the author's polemic.
- Image 2: the punctuation around the anti-Hindi question and the Madurai temple priests is awkward. The English treats the temple priests as the subject of the subsequent temple-entry question, without attaching them to the preceding anti-Hindi question or supplying an unnamed actor there. The sentence about the Self-Respect Movement continues onto Image 3.
- Image 3: the Upanishad passage is explicitly reported as something “they say”; that qualification is retained. No specific Upanishad, verse number or external quotation is substituted for the printed excerpt. “Song” retains the metaphor in `புரட்சிக் கீதம்`; the English does not assert a separate musical composition.

- Part 5 Images 4–5: the author's explicit distinction between Brahmin people and the system he calls Brahminism is preserved throughout. The demographic proportions and claims about inherited religious authority are the author's argument, not independently supplied findings. `வகுப்பு` is “community” in this caste context.
- Image 4: `சூட்சுமம்` is “crux,” referring here to the underlying explanation. `சொந்தமான` retains its quotation marks as “belonging,” preserving the claim that the issue also concerns non-Brahmin supporters.
- Image 5: `படைபலம்` is “armed strength,” retaining the military metaphor for accumulated support. `மயங்கும் வேளையிலே` is interpreted as “in a moment of delusion” in the sorcerer comparison, not as a diagnosed medical condition. `மோட்சம்` is “salvation”; the nose-rope, bridle, breath and bombing comparisons remain the source's rhetorical illustrations.

- Part 5 Image 6: the pluralized names `ஆனந்தாச்சாரிகள்` and `அவினாசிகள்` are retained as “the Anandacharis” and “the Avinashis,” without assigning specific biographical identities not supplied here. They illustrate the author's distinction between Brahmin and non-Brahmin responses.
- `தூத்துக்குடி` is retained as the heading “Thoothukudi.” The following chained figure is an allegory, explicitly identified as the Dravidian at the end of Image 7; no external myth or named prisoner is imported.
- Image 7: `அபயக் குரல்` is a “reassuring voice,” conveying a promise of safety. `சிந்து` remains “sindhu song,” retaining the song-form reference. The literal iron chains and figurative heart of steel retain the source's contrast. The closing sentence about the Dravidian continues onto Image 8.

- Part 5 Images 8–9: the chained figure is identified as the Dravidian in Image 7; the liberator is explicitly identified as Periyar in Image 9. The allegorical army, bare-handed blows and shattering fetters are retained. `வெள்ளை உள்ளம்` is “innocent heart”; `சம்மட்டி` is “sledgehammer.”
- Image 9: the argument that ancient fetters must have weakened is the speaker's metaphor, not an added factual claim about particular materials. “Thirty years” and “last year” remain the source's relative time references, without converting them into inferred dates. `திராவிடம்` is “Dravidam,” distinct from the person “the Dravidian” in the preceding allegory. The crowd figure is translated as printed: more than a hundred thousand.
- The final comparison between parties searching for recruits and the Dravidar Kazhagam gathering continues onto Image 10.

- Part 5 Image 10: the quoted couplet is translated from the retained source without importing an attribution or variant lyric. The recurring grass-bearing hand is retained literally; its ritual association is reinforced by the next scan's reference to wearers of the three-stranded thread. The tiger and sword remain the author's polemical images. The unusual `யோசிக்கச் செல்கிறோம்` is rendered contextually as “urge them to think”; the Tamil is unchanged.
- Part 6 Image 1: `முப்புரியினர்` is “wearers of the three-stranded thread,” retaining the sacred-thread reference. *Bharata Devi* remains the publication title; “the 11th” is not expanded into an inferred month or year.
- The source shifts from `ஆசாபாசங்கள்` (“desires and attachments”) to `ஆபாசங்கள்` / `ஆபாசம்` (“vulgarities” / “vulgarity”). The English preserves this lexical difference rather than silently harmonizing the later forms with the earlier word. Political silkworms and grasshoppers retain the source's imagery. The final quoted challenge continues onto Image 2.

- Part 6 Image 2: “half a crore or a crore” retains the source's numbering (five million or ten million); the source supplies no currency unit here. `மாற்றுக் குறையாத` retains the gold-purity metaphor. `முத்தண்ணாக் கூட்டம்` remains “Muthanna crowd,” a polemical collective label without an added specific personal identification; “born from the face” retains the quoted origin claim.
- The grass-bearing hand here turns to a bow (`வில்`), unlike the preceding sword (`வாள்`); the change is preserved. The internal-differences passage's `இடையே உள்ளவர்கள்` is rendered “the people between them,” retaining its somewhat unclear scope rather than supplying an inferred faction or named individuals.
- Part 6 Image 3: “assembly of the world” translates `உலக மன்றம்` without naming a particular international institution. The khadi shirt remains a concrete image. The two indented declarations preserve the source's distinct emphatic passages. The final sentence about the “eye of wisdom” is syntactically extended from the opening words of Image 4 and continues there without repetition.

- Part 6 Image 4: `மரண சாசனம்` is “Last Will and Testament.” The ensuing exhortation concerns the author's political struggle and anticipated persecution. The personification of Varnashrama and the mountain-blasting comparison remain the source's rhetoric. `வர்ணாஸ்ரமம்` is retained as “Varnashrama,” the caste-and-life-stage social order.
- In the phrase `நாம் இருக்குமட்டும் நமது ஆதிக்கத்துக்கு ஆபத்துதான்`, the printed possessive is literally “our dominance” although the surrounding personification points to Varnashrama's dominance. The English uses “its dominance” as an explicit contextual interpretation; the Tamil is not altered. “Last week” remains relative; no date or independently reconstructed account of the Vaigai disturbance is added.
- Image 5: the full catalogue of persecution is retained, including `சிறுத்தை` as “leopards” and `கழுவிலேற்றுதல்` as impalement. No unnamed victim or event is identified by conjecture. The contrast between dying and being killed, and the resulting figurative immortality, belongs to the author's argument. The final personification of death continues onto Image 6.

- Part 6 Image 6: the hymn excerpt is translated only from the retained Tamil; no unprinted author or source is assigned. `தோட்டம் துரவு` is “gardens and wells.” The elliptical question ending `எண்ணம் குறையப் போகிறதோ` is interpreted as rejecting a retreat into property-related worries; its wording is awkward and the Tamil remains unchanged.
- `மாணசாசனம்` in the second paragraph is rendered contextually as “last wills and testaments,” matching the repeated `மரண சாசனம்` elsewhere; the anomalous source spelling is retained. Kudanthai remains the printed place-name rather than being silently replaced. “The week after Madurai” is kept relative without adding a date.
- Image 7: the statements about death, family attachments and sacrifice are the author's concluding exhortation. They are translated in full without adding present-day advice. `அஸ்திரம்` is “weapon.” The work ends with the assurance that the ideal will triumph; no missing continuation is implied.

- Part 2 Image 8: the unresolved epithet `சுனைவிழுங்கிகள்` is retained in the English as “sunai-vizhungis,” alongside the clear description “wriggling in their holes.” Its precise idiomatic meaning remains uncertain; no conjectural English insult is substituted.
